2214. JOHN BLEWITT was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of August, 2 weights, value 1 s., the goods of William James Whitehead.
WILLIAM JAMES WHITEHEAD . I am a baker, and live at Woolwich. I know the prisoner, he has been in the habit of coming to my shop—I had missed weights before—the last time he came was on the 1st of September about a quarter before eleven o'clock, for a halfpenny-worth or penny-worth of bread—I quitted the shop—the weights were left in the shop—I missed them at ten minutes past eleven o'clock and saw them again on Friday, the 2nd of September, between one and two o'clock—I knew them to be mine.
SARAH BURBAGE . I live at Woolwich. I purchased these weights of the prisoner—I had bought things of him before—to the best of my knowledge
I gave 4 d. for the weights—I cannot tell when that was—I do not know when he was taken up.
WILLIAM CAMPION . I am the officer. I received information, and took the prisoner to Sarah Burbage to see if he was the person who sold her the weights—she said he was.
JURY to WILLIAM JAMES WHITEHEAD. Q. Is there any mark on these weights? A. Yes—the bottom of one of them being chipped to make way for the stamp to go in—I do not know whether any one else had been in my shop.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18360919-2215]
2215. JOHN BLEWITT was again indicted for stealing, on the 30th of August, 7 weights, value 11 s., the goods of Myers Joseph.
JULIA JOSEPH . I am the wife of Myers Joseph, and live at Woolwich On the 30th of August I saw the prisoner at my shop—I saw him take seven weights, and put them into his pocket—I kept him, and called out for assistance—a young man came to me—he knocked me down, and ran away—a young man caught him—these are the weights.
SIMON JACOB . I was in the workshop when the prisoner was in the shop, and my mistress called for assistance—I came up, and the prisoner knocked my mistress down, and threw part of the weights on the ground—I caught hold of him—he dragged me a little distance, and a neighbour came out, and said I should let him alone—the prisoner bit my finger, and escaped—we gave information, and he was taken on Thursday or Friday—I am sure he is the same.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Transported for Seven Years more.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)

[trialAccount t18360919-2216]
2216. JOHN MCCLUSTER was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of August, 1 pair of trowsers, value 4 s.; 1 jacket, value 4 s.; and 1 waistcoat, value 2 s.; the goods of William George Bracegirdle.
WILLIAM GEORGE BRACEGIRDLE . I am a smack-owner, living at Greenwich. I had a jacket and trowsers on board the smack Laurel on the Thames—I saw them safe on the night of the 2nd of August, at ten o'clock, and the boy went to bed—while he was on board, these clothes were stolen—I missed them the next morning—these are them.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. How many boys might there be on board this vessel? A. Two or three—I bought these clothes for my apprentice—the prisoner denied having them—as far as I know I was not present when he was taken up.
JAMES PARRY ( police-sergeant R 8.) I found the trowsers at Mr. Harber's, the pawnbroker, at Greenwich.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he not give up the duplicates at once, and say another boy employed him to pawn them? A. Yes.
COURT. Q. Did you take him? A. Yes; and charged him with it—I asked him if he pledged them—he said, "Yes"—I asked him where he got them from—he said from a boy named Thomas Everett, who, he said, was gone to Scotland.
COURT to WILLIAM GEORGE BRACEGIRDLE. Q. Have you seen the prisoner before? A. Yes; at times, but not no board the vessel—he is an apprentice to Mr. Bromfield, a ship-owner.
EDWIN BOLTON . The prisoner pledged these things with me, in the name of John Carter, on the morning of the 4th of August—here is the corresponding duplicate.
Prisoner's Defence. One morning I was going down the street, and met this boy—he said, "Will you do me a favour?"—I said, "I don't mind"—he said, "Here is a jacket and trowsers"—I took them to the pawn-shop, it was not open, I took them to a house, and I took them again, I took half the money with me to sea—his name was Tom Everett—he is gone to sea—I have never seen him since.
JURY to WILLIAM GEORGE BRACEGIRDLE. Q. Was your vessel in such a situation that any other person might get on board from the shore? A. Yes; they might—there is occasionally a person on board to see who comes—from three to four people could have come on board without notice that day—in the morning, when the boy was called to come to his work, he had to send to me for things—during the time of low-water the vessel was dry, and any body could have come on board.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Transported for Seven Years.
SURREY LARCENIES.
Before Mr. Recorder.

2217. WILLIAM DWIGHT was indicted for embezzlement.
SARAH RYDER . I am the wife of William Ryder. The prisoner was in his employ on the 27th of June, to take out tares and sell them in London—he was not to give any credit—he took out 30 bundles, and was to return with 13 s. 4 d.—when he returned I was present, and he brought back only 1 s. 7 d.,—my husband asked him where the remainder of the money was—he said he did not know—my husband said then he ought to know, and he must learn him better—the prisoner said my husband might get the money how he could—we have no witness to show that he sold the tares—my husband saw them loaded on the cart—he used to be trusted to sell them, and bring the money home—he has regular customers to sell them to—they are not here—he never said he sold the tares—he produced 1 s. 7 d., and said it was all we should have.
EDWARD SIMS . I am a constable, and took the prisoner into custody, I apprehended him at Hounslow, and told him he was my prisoner—he said he was glad I had come after him, and he would give himself up, and owned he was wrong, and owned to embezzling the money—he said, "I own I am wrong, and owned to embezzle the money; I am myself in fault"—those were the words—he wished to compromise it; if he could see the prosecutor he would make good the money—I did not ask him if he had sold them—I asked him no questions—I cannot exactly say his words.
JURY Q. Did he tell you he had sold the tares? A. Yes—some he had sold, and some he trusted—he did not say to what amount—he did not tell me he had received 11 s. 9 d. in money—he owned he had done wrong.
NOT GUILTY .

2219. JAMES BLOOM was indicted for embezzlement.

HENRY HERRINGTON . I received a bedstead from the prosecutor, by the hands of the prisoner, on the 24th of August, and paid him a sovereign—he gave me a bill and receipt.

2220. BENJAMIN BOLTON was indicted for stealing on the 14th of September, 8lbs. of bacon, value 4 s., the goods of Harry Izod.
GEORGE DANBY . I am servant to Mr. Harry Izod, of Blackman-street On Wednesday, the 14th of September, I saw the prisoner there—he took the bacon and ran away with it—I ran, and overtook him in the Borough—I asked him where the bacon was—he said he knew nothing of it—I took him back to the shop—he fell on his knees, and confessed it—he said he was very poor.
Prisoner. Q. What did I do after I stole the bacon? A. You ran away—I saw you take it off a cheese—I do not know what you did with it—you had not got it when I took you—you came out of an alley.
JOHN MORRIS . On the 14th of September I saw the prisoner, between four and five o'clock, being pursued by Danby—I saw him turn the corner, and I picked the bacon up about twenty yards behind him—I did not see it in his possession, but I picked it up in the direction he was running.
Prisoner's Defence. I know nothing about it—I had not the bacon—I am a pensioner, and had just come up from Greenwich.
Guilty.
(The indictment also charged him with previous conviction.)
WILLIAM ROSS ( police-constable M 86.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from the Clerk of the Peace—( read.)—I was present at his trial, and am sure he is the man.
GUILTY . Aged 65.— Confined Six Months.

2221. GEORGE BASSETT was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of September, 8 knives, value 5 s.; and 6 forks, value 5 s.; the goods of Alfred Stanes Pigeon, his master.
HARRY GREEN . I am in the service of Alfred Stanes Pigeon, a distiller, opposite St. George's Church, Borough. The prisoner was his servants—he was in the habit of going up stairs daily to clean knives, forks, boots, and shoes—on the 11th of September I found two small knives concealed under
the staircase—the prisoner was in the house at the time—he left at eight o'clock—he did not sleep there—after he left I looked, and the knives were gone—on Monday, the 12th, he was watched, and some knives were missed from the number given him to clean—I missed one knife and one fork, and found them in the place where the other two were—we let them remain there till the prisoner went away: at ten minutes after eight o'clock in the evening, he was going out of the house, I stopped him, and told him I thought he had got them on his person—he denied it several times, but at last produced them from his pocket.
STEPHEN TWOHIG . I am a policeman. The prisoner was given into my custody—I learnt from him where his lodgings were, and went to them, and found seven knives and five forks.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
(The prisoner put in a written Defence, stating that he expected a few friends to visit him, and, not having sufficient knives, he took these home, intending to return them.)
GUILTY. Aged 24.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Justice Bosanquet.
[trialAccount t18360919-2222]
2222. MARY BROMLEY was indicted for a robbery on Mary Ann Hall, on the 13th of August, putting her in fear, and taking from her person, and against her will, 1 shawl, value 5 s.; 1 half-crown, and 2 shillings; the goods and monies of George Hall.
MARY ANN HALL . I am the wife of George Hall, a sawyer; he lives at a timber wharf in the Commercial-road. On a Saturday in August, I was passing along Waterloo-road, near the New Cut—I had a cloak on my right arm—I saw the prisoner there, with some men and woman—my cloak touched another young woman, but not the prisoner—she struck me directly several blows on my left eye, and called me dreadful names—I lost my senses for a few minutes—I did not fall down—I fell against a post—when I recovered I missed my shawl off my shoulder, and 4 s. 6 d. was gone out of my hand—it was loose in my hand—I was going out to buy a little meat, I never wear a pocket, I always carry my money in my hand—I did not say or do any thing when I missed it—she ill-treated me and kicked me—the people were gone when I came to my senses—I have not seen my shawl or money again—I did not give her in charge till two hours after.
Prisoner. She gave me in charge at ten o'clock, and said she had lost 4 s. 6 d.; and when the policeman came up, she said she could not swear I took it out of her hand, but she had lost it—did you not call in names? Witness. I never spoke to her—I did not give her in charge for two hours after.
SUSAN BABER . I am the wife of Chi✗ Baber, a shoe-maker. I was passing by the end of Waterloo-road, and saw the prisoner and other people the prosecutrix was before me—she had a cloak on her right arm, and a shawl on her shoulders—Mrs. Hall's cloak touched one of the females who were passing, but not the prisoner—it pushed against her as she passed as there was a crowd of people passing, and she was pushed nearly off the pavement—the prisoner began abusing her, calling her very ill names, and struck her several times—then she struck her a blow with the right hand in the left eye, and took her shawl off her shoulder with her right hand—she handed the shawl to another female, of the name of Hannah Flynn, and said, "Take this, and I will pitch into her again," and she struck her
several times afterwards—I did not know Flynn, but the prisoner called her by that name—I went home then—I afterwards went with Mrs. Hall to the New Cut, and saw the prisoner standing opposite the Victoria Theatre—she was given into custody by Mrs. Hall—she called Mrs. Hall very ill names—Mrs. Hall told her, if she would give up her shawl, she would not give her in charge, but if she did not give it up she would—she made a dreadful oath, and said she had not got the shawl, but she had had the worth of it.
Prisoner. Q. Did you see me take the shawl? A. Yes—I did not interfere at all till Mrs. Hall came to me—she fainted away against my shoulder, and took hold of one of the lamp-posts.
THOMAS HODGSKIN . I am a policeman. The prisoner was given into my charge between twelve and one o'clock in the morning, in the New Cut, for stealing the shawl—she denied all knowledge of the robbery.
Prisoner. I gave myself in charge. Witness. She did not; the prosecutrix gave her in charge.
Prisoner's Defence. The prisoner came up and pushed against me, and called me a bumper —I said I was no more a bumper than she was, and she struck against me, and I struck her again—she then gave me in charge of George Smith—he asked if I had robbed her—she said no, she had lost 4 s. 6 d., but could not say I robbed her—and between twelve and one o'clock she came and asked me for her shawl—I said I had not got it—she said, "I shall give you in charge; I can't swear to the money, but I can swear to the shawl;" and went for a policeman.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Justice Bosanquet.
[trialAccount t18360919-2223]
2223. JOHN HAMILTON and HENRY KNIGHT were indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of George Revell, on the 25th of August, at St. Mary, Newington, and stealing therein 2 pieces, containing 12 yards, of lawn, value 1 l. 9 s., his goods.
CHARLES OUTHWAITE . I am shopman to George Revell, who occupies a house in the parish of St. Mary, Newington, in Surrey. On the 25th of August, a few minutes before twelve o'clock in the day, I saw both the prisoners standing at the window for two or three minutes together—Hamilton put his thumb down to the glass, cracked it, pushed the piece in, and took out two pieces of lawn—Knight was standing within two or three feet of him at the time—when I first saw them they were together—when Hamilton took the lawn they walked away different ways—I immediately ran out at the private door, and caught Hamilton—I bought him into the house—he was searched by a policeman, who found the two pieces of lawn on him which I had seen taken out of the window—the policeman took Knight—I am sure of their persons.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. What time of day was it? A. Twelve o'clock—I saw them at the shop window—I ran out immediately I saw what Hamilton did—Knight was two or three yards off when Hamilton was taken, he was not more than six yards off—I saw him taken afterwards—I am sure he is the same person—my attention at first was more directed to the man who was breaking the window, but I saw both—I will swear Knight is the person.
Hamilton. He said at the office that the window was broken before. Witness. I understand it was cracked a little, but not so much as when he cracked it further—I did not myself know it was cracked—I cannot swear it was not, but I heard it cracked still further.
COURT. Q. Was it open? A. No, nor yet entirely cracked across—there was no hole in the window—it was not possible for a person to put his hand in through the window without cracking it still further.
JAMES HANDLEY . I am porter to the prosecutor. I was in the shop about twelve o'clock, and saw the two prisoners standing near the window for two or three minutes—I did not see them speak to each other—I saw Hamilton put his hand down to the window and force a piece of glass in—it was cracked a little before, but it was not loose, it was quite tight, the edges of the glass touched each other—he forced the piece in, and took the two pieces of lawn out—I immediately ran out at the side door, and caught Hamilton—I saw Knight walk by—when I caught Hamilton he was quite close to him—I brought Hamilton back, and the lawn was found on him—Knight was taken by a policeman, two or three minutes after.
EDWARD BETTS . I am a policeman. I received Hamilton in charge—I did not see him till he was in custody—I received the lawn, and have it here.
JAMES ANDREWS . I am a policeman. I was on duty in Blackman-street about twelve o'clock, and saw Hamilton, Knight, and another one cross from Suffolk-street to the end of Trinity-street—I instantly stood still, and had scarcely done so, when all on a sudden, a disturbance took place—they were going towards the prosecutor's shop window then—they stood at the window a minute or two—I crossed over, and saw Hamilton in the custody of the two witnesses—I said, "Hold him fast, I will go after the other"—I went after Knight, who was walking away, and took him—he merely said, "What do you want with me?"
Cross-examined. Q. What distance was Knight from Hamilton when you took him? A. I suppose about a dozen yards—he was walking from the shop.
COURT. Q. How near was he to Hamilton when you saw him going towards the shop? A. As close as he possibly could be—all three went together in company.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
Knight's Defence. On being taken into custody by Andrew, he tapped me on the shoulder; he searched me, and found 10 s. 2 d., a small handkerchief, and a duplicate of a coat. I told him it was my coat, and I was going to get it out of pawn in the Dover-road, and that was in the way to the pawnbroker's.
HAMILTON— GUILTY . Aged 28.— Transported for Life.
KNIGHT— NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18360919-2224]
2224. HENRY KNIGHT again indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of John Orrick Kennard, on the 25th of August, and stealing therein 70 handkerchiefs, value 12 l., his goods.
JOHN ORRICK KENNARD . I occupy a house at St. Mary, Newington. I left my shop on the morning of the 25th of August, about half-past eight o'clock, and returned at about half-past eleven—before I left I had about 25 l. worth of silk handkerchiefs in the centre of the window, about a foot or a foot and a half from the glass—the window was quite safe—none of the glass was broken—when I returned, my wife was standing at the door, and gave me information—I saw the glass ha been broken, and missed about half the quantity of handkerchiefs which had been in the window—I judge them to be worth about 12 l.—the best of them were taken—I am confident they were worth more than 5 l. or 10 l.—I have seen
one of the handkerchiefs since, which I know to be the same pattern—they were in separate quantities of one, two, three, or five, or six—some were single, and the others different quantities.
JAMES DAY . I am just turned ten years of age. I stand in the street with my father, selling oysters—I was going past Mr. Kennard's shop on the day in question, I saw Knight put his hand into window and take the handkerchiefs out—I did not see the window broken—I saw that it was broken before he put his hand through—I cannot tell how many he took out—he put his hand in twice—there was some one with him—I did not notice the other—I knew Knight by sight before—I am sure he is the person—I was going on an errand—I went on—I then went back and told my father, and he told me to go and tell Mr. Kennard.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Were you close to him? A. Yes; I stopped and saw his hand go in twice—there were two persons by, not more—it was the prisoner who put his hand in, not the other man—he ran away—I saw him the next Monday—this was Thursday—I am quite sure Knights the man—I saw my father about two minutes after.
COURT. Q. About what o'clock was it? A. About half-past ten.
JAMES ANDREWS . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner into custody, in Trinity-street, on another charge, about twelve o'clock—I found a small handkerchief on him, which I produce.
Cross-examined. Q. When he was shown to Day, was any body else in custody? A. I believe he was in the cell, and that the boy saw him the first day—I cannot say whether any body was with him—he might be alone.
JOHN ORRICK KENNARD re-examined. This handkerchief is the same pattern as I lost—I have no shop mark on it.
Cross-examined. Q. Were there any better than that one? A. Most of them were larger than this, and cost me from 3 s. 6 d. to 3 s. 9 d.—I have no mark on it—the boy identified Knight at the station-house—I think it was the first day he was taken—he was taken to one cell, and immediately said, "That is him"—he was taken to another cell, and asked if that was one, and said, "No".
Prisoner's Defence. The shopman in the last case said at the office he saw me, Hamilton, and another, lurking about the window, that we left at nine o'clock, and returned at twelve and committed the robbery; the boy says it was half-past ten o'clock when he saw me commit the robbery; I can call George Wickers, a policeman, to swear he saw me come from my home between half-past ten o'clock and a quarter to eleven which is a mile and a half from the shop. Wyatt can prove I was at home at half-past ten o'clock; it was within a few minutes of the time I left home with the money to get the things out of pawn.
JAMES ANDREWS re-examined. Revell and Kennard's shops are about a mile apart—I first saw knight in company with the others about five minutes to twelve o'clock, but I saw them before that, about nine o'clock, as I was coming from home, in Blackman-street, close by Revell's shop, going towards Walworth, and towards Kennard's.
ELIZABETH WYATT . I live at No. 31, Charles-street, Oakley-street, Westminster-road, My husband works at Alder's cement-wharf—the prisoner lives in the same house as me—he was at home about half-past ten o'clock in the morning, I know, brushing his shoes—he had a cost in pledge, which his mother gave him four half-crowns and sixpence to fetch out, and she gave him the ticket—he left soon after half-past ten o'clock—I heard him come down stairs, and, as I suppose, go out—I cannot
exactly say the time—I did not know where the coat was in pledge—Mr. Kennard's shop is about a mile and a half or two miles from Charles-street.
GUILTY Stealing in the dwelling house, but not of breaking and entering, — Transported for Life.
Before Mr. Justice Bosanquet.

2226. WILLIAM WITHERS indicted for a misdemeanour.
MR. CHAMBERS conducted the Prosecution.
EMMA HIBBERT . I live in Bedford-place. Borough-road. On the 13th of September I was walking in Blackfriars-road, near the New Cut, and saw the prisoner there—my sister Rose was there with me—we all three went into a public-house—he accompanied us to our lodging, and went up stairs with us—he remained in the room with me—my sister was constantly in and out—he proposed to drink tea with us—I said I could not afford it, but if he would send half a crown down to the landlady, she would send tea up—he pulled a parcel out of his pocket, which seemed to me to contain half-sovereigns—my sister was in the room at the time he gave me two half-crowns, which I took down to the landlady—I shortly afterwards left him alone with my sister, for nearly half an hour, and when I came back she had got half-a-sovereign—she showed it to me—I said it was not a good one—she sounded it on the drawers, and said it was good—the prisoner said he wished he had got a thousand of them, and that it was good—I asked him to give me one, and, after considering, he said he could not afford it, but at last said, if I would fetch the 5 s. back, he would give me one—I fetched him the 5 s., and gave them to him, and he gave me what I supposed was half-a-sovereign—he left the room almost immediately, and said he would be back in a quarter of an hour—after he was gone, I rubbed the half-sovereign between my fingers, and thought it was not good—I went down stairs and saw the landlady, and then went out in pursuit of him—I found him about two doors, below, on the opposite side of the way—I told him he had given me bad money—he said, had he? he would give me good, and asked me to give him that I had got in my hand—I said, "No"—he said if I would give it to him, he would give me good, and asked if I would have it all in gold, or all in silver—I said I did not care what it was, so that it was good—he walked a good way—I turned round to look for a policeman—we walked a long distance—he asked me if I would accompany him to his place, and then he would give it me all in silver—he went to snatch both half-sovereigns, and snatched one from my hand—Wyborn and two policeman came up—I saw him then take out of his right-hand pocket a brown paper parcel and throw it into the road—it was found to contain several gilt sixpences—I gave the one I had in my hand, to Corstorpham the constable.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was not he making a great display of all this parcel of gilt sixpences? A. He run them through his hand, from his right hand into his left—he walked with me through the street—I am twenty-four years old—I had a quartern of gin, which he paid for.
ROSE HIBBERT . I have heard my sister's statement, it is correct—I received a half-sovereign, which I gave her,
JOHN CHANNER . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner into custody—I did not see him throw any thing away—Emma Hibbert gave me a gilt sixpence.
EDWARD MARSHALL . I am a policeman. I searched the prisoner, and found on him a sovereign, two half-crowns, four shillings, a sixpence, and some copper money—all good.
RICHARD WYBORN . I saw the prisoner throw the parcel away—I picked it up, and gave it to the inspector.
DAVID CORSTORPHAM . I produce the money. The prisoner was brought into my custody at the station-house—he said the charge was all false; I was a conspiracy on the part of the girls and the policeman against him—that he did not give them any bad money, nor had he ever possession of the money which was picked up.
JOHN FIELD . I am Inspector of counterfeit coin to the Mint. The whole of these are gilt sixpences—they are dated 1824 and 1825—there are bout the same quantity of each—they are gilt, to resemble half-sovereigns.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe, in consequence of a representation made to the Home Office, you went down to Wycombe? A. I went to make inquiry into the bail which was offered—I found the prisoner was a small shoe-maker, keeping a shop there—I understand he has children.
( MR. PHILLIPS addressed the Jury for the prisoner, who received a good character.) GUILTY.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18360919-2227]
2227. EDWARD HALES indicted for stealing, on the 12th of August, 1 pair of leather half-fronts for boots, value 2 s., the goods of William Martin, his master.
HENRY NORRIS . I am warehouseman to William Martin, a leather-seller, who lives in Union-street, Borough. The prisoner was his porter—I directed him, on Friday evening, the 12th of August, to get some corn for the horses—he was gone longer than he ought to have been—I went to an where he was, and found him behind the counter, where he had no rights as be—I missed a pair of half-fronts of boots—I charged him with it the next morning—h acknowledged he had them, and said he took them to a person to be made for his own feet—they have not been found.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. You missed others? A. We took stock, and missed forty-six pairs.
COURT. Q. About this last pair, what did you do? A. I charged him with it—he at first denied it, but I told him I was positive he had them—he at last said he had taken them.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you tell him if he would pay you 2 s. for them, you would put them down and say nothing about it? A. In the first instance I did—I asked whether he had them—he said he had the forty-six pairs—I never told him I would forgive him, nor did any body in my presence—I appointed him to come to my house after he left his master's, to see if I could discover whether he had the forty-six pairs or not—I did not say, "If you tell the truth, you shall not have any thing done to you"—we took three or four persons into custody about the fronts, and
took the prisoner before the Magistrate I do not consider he was a witness, he was not sworn—he was placed at the bar—he went, at my request, top three different houses, to point out the people who bought them—I did not, in the policeman's presence, promise him that nothing should be done to him—he went, knowing himself guilty, I suppose—I asked him and the policeman likewise to go.
JOHN COLLINS ( police-constable M 176.) I took the prisoner—I said nothing to him on the subject—as soon as he was given into custody, he said he would show me where he sold them—he took me and Norris to a house where he said he had sold three pairs for 2 s.
Cross-examined. Q. Was there any offer made to him? A. Not in my presence—this is the first time I have heard of any—Norris said, "I give this prisoner into custody for robing his employer of boot-fronts"—I was sent for to Mr. Norris's house—the prisoner came there with one of Mr. Norris's lads—I did not learn from Norris that he had told the prisoner if he would tell the truth no harm should come to him—he told him what he had done with the boots—I did not tell him he need not answer—he took me to the right places.
Prisoner. He said, "You will have nothing done to you if you tell Mr. Martin the whole truth, "and I came up about eight o'clock on Tuesday morning.
MR. CLARKSON to HENRY NORRIS. Q. How long had he been in your master's service? A. I think about four months—he had 10 s. a week, and a good many perquisites besides—he takes out a good deal of leather, and he generally had something of the parties to whom it was taken.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 19.— Recommend to mercy. — Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18360919-2228]
2228. GEORGE HAYMAN indicted for stealing, on the 5th of September, 20 shillings, and sixpences, the monies of William Edward Sawyer, his master.
WILLIAM EDWARD SAWYER . I am a newspaper-agent, and live at St. Mary, Newington. The prisoner was in my service—on the 5th of September I gave him 33 s. to go and purchase some papers—he did not return—I found him lying in the passage of a house—I asked him what he had done with the money—he said he had lost it—I took him to his house, and asked him how he had got the new boots which he had on—he said he lost part of the money—I said he could not lose part without all—he then said he would tell me the truth, that he had bought the boots, he had been to the theatre, and to mother Rice's, and 5 s. he was robbed of—I gave him 20 shillings, and 26 sixpences.
GUILTY . Aged 14.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18360919-2229]
2229. JAMES ELSGOOD and THOMAS ROOTS were indicted for stealing, on the 8th of September, 3 1/2 bushels of clover, bran, and beans, mixed together, value 2 s. 6 d. the goods of William Carpenter, the master of James Elsgood.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
ROBERT BUTCHER . I am clerk to Mr. Carpenter, a miller, at Greenwich. Elsgood was his carter—I only know Roots since he was taken up—it was the duty of Elsgood to take out flour to deliver to the customers,
but not to collect empty bags without he received orders—he had 14 s. a week, and 1 d. a sack on what he took out, amounting to from 2 l. to 2 l. 4 s. a week—he had the care of his master's horses—they have fallen off in flesh very much lately—they were fed with clover, bran, and beans—in consequence of their looking thin I increased the quantity of food, but they did not at all improve—on the 8th of September Elsgood went out with the wagon, and had directions to call at Mr. Grigg's for some sacks—I took out a sample from the stock of food in the bin, and I saw a sample produced by the policeman—they are both here—they correspond in all respects—I had spoken to the policeman before.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. What is it composed of? A. Clover, bran, and beans—that is generally our mixture—there is nothing uncommon in it—my master is not here.
JAMES WILD ( police-constable 141.) On the 8th of September, by the direction of Butcher, I followed the prisoner Elsgood's wagon—he had three nose-bags hanging under the hind part of the axletree, which appeared to be full—I saw a boy named Kneller—he met Elsgood's wagon at the Broadway, Deptford, just as he set out, at five o'clock in the morning—I followed the wagon to several places, till it got to Old Gravel-lane—Elsgood there emptied the nose-bags into a flour-sack, and as he tied it up I noticed a darn in it—he threw it down, and sat on it—he then went on to the hay market, in Whitechapel, where a country cart drew alongside of his wagon, and put some hay into it—Elsgood then drove on to the New Cross Inn—I there saw Roots standing outside, near the watering-trough—they spoke together, but I did not hear what they said—then Roots took the full sack from the shafts, and took it into his stable—Elsgood was feeding his horses with hay, and watering them—Roots was not gone more than two or three minutes—he then brought the empty sack, and put it where he took it from—he then went into the public-house and got a pot of beer—they drank, and the boy had some—I got assistance, and we went into the stable—I there found the food I produce—Elsgood had taken the sack away—I let him go on—I went to Mr. Carpenter's, and found the same sack there—from the time I saw Roots go into the stable till I went is, no one else went in—I found between three and four bushels—I at last found the sack in Mr. Carpenter's yard, along with some others—I took Elsgood into custody, and told him what for—he said it was a b----y lie.
Cross-examined. Q. What boy is this you were talking of? A. He is here—I was in Red Lion-street—Elsgood did not hang the none-bags on the horses' heads for three quarters of an hour, nor did he at all—the boy was sometimes on a-head with the horses, sometimes he drove the wagon—he had an opportunity of seeing whether the bags were put on the horses—this is the contents of the three nose-bags.
JAMES KNELLER . I was with these horses and wagon that day—I met Elsgood first just through the Broadway—I was in Red Lion-street with him—I did not see him put any nose-bags to the horses' heads.
Elsgood's Defence. I am quite innocent—as for Thomas Roots taking this off the shafts, it is false—he never came near it.
Roots' Defence. I was standing by a gentleman's horse—Elsgood came and asked for a pot of beer—I went and got it, and put it by his horses—that policeman is a false-swearing man.
ELSGOOD— GUILTY . Aged 24.— Transported for Seven Years.
ROOTS— GUILTY . Aged 36.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18360919-2230]
2230. FREDERICK BROUGHTON was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of September, 1 breast-pin, value 8 l., the goods of John Price, from his person.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN PRICE . I carry on business in Tower-street. On Thursday last I was at a subscription skittle-ground, at the Mason's Arms, in Horsemonger-lane—I played at skittles—the prisoner came in after had been there some time—I had been drinking—I left off playing about half-past nine o'clock, and sat down on a bench on the right side of the ground, and went to sleep—my diamond pin was then in the breast of my shirt, and when I awoke it was gone—I received information, and on going to the bar I saw my pin in the possession of Cook—this is it.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You had been drinking? A. I had been dining with some friends—I am afraid I was tipsy—it was a covered ground where I sat down—I felt the pin safe and secure—I had drank wine, and brandy and water—I do not believe I touched any beer—I should say the prisoner was not particularly tipsy.
WILLIAM JOHNSON . I am employed in these grounds to set up the skittles—I saw the prosecutor there and the prisoner—I saw Mr. Price fall asleep—I saw his diamond pin in his shirt—after he was asleep I saw the prisoner sit down by the side of him, then put his hand up to his breast and take the pin out gently—he sat down there for half a minute—he then crossed to the other side—I did not se what he did with it—I looked at the prosecutor's shirt at the time, and saw that the pin was gone—I let the prisoner be in the room five minutes, and stuck up the skittles again—I then ran out and called my master, who came up to him, and I saw the pin taken from the prisoner by Cook.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you say a word about this till the prisoner had gone away? A. No—I swear he did not pick it up off the ground—I have been there five months—my father is an attendant in Horsemonger-lane—I have been there to do things for my father—I was not turned out and desired not to come again—I do not go there now—I was turned out for a day, about a year and a half ago.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Were you ever turned out of any place for dishonesty? A. No; never.
MATTHEW SEYMOUR IONN . I keep the Masons' Arms, to which this skittle ground is attached. Johnson came to me that night, and gave me information about the prisoner—I went to the prisoner, and met him coming out of the skittle-ground, while the boy was telling me the particulars—I said to him, "You have got Mr. Price's pin"—he said he had not—I took hold of his right hand, which was in his pocket, and just at that time Cook, the officer, came up, and I gave the prisoner in charge—I drew his hand out of his pocket, and he had the pin in it.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you not first say, "Is it true that you have taken Mr. Price's pin?" A. I did; I spoke to him four times—I should say he was a little tipsy.
THOMAS COOK . I am a constable. I came up and took the prisoner's hand from his pocket—Mr. Ionn said, "You have taken Mr. Price's pin"—he said, "I have not; is is not likely a gentleman of my respectability would be guilty of such a thing"—he produced nothing—we were obliged to draw it from his pocket—I told him I was on officer, and must see if he had the pin—he still denied it.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you an officer? A. Yes; I am assistant to Mr. Garland, the high constable—I do the business at Mr. John's house—I am a bricklayer by trade—when I took the pin from the prisoner he told me he had picked it up off the ground—I had forgotten that.
Prisoner. I found the pin; I picked it up.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 20.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined Three Months; the last week solitary.
[trialAccount t18360919-2231]
2231. WILLIAM PEARCE and ELIZABETH REVELL were indicted for stealing, on the 19th of September, 3 bottles, value 9 d.; and 1 1/2 pint of wine, value 2 s.; the goods of John Gregory, their master.
JOHN GREGORY. I live in Newington-causeway, and am an oilman. The prisoners were my servants—Pearce was my porter, and Revell a servant in the house—I have lost some bottles, and a pint and a half of wine—this is the wine and bottles—I sell British wines—the value of this is 2 s., bottles and all—they were found on the prisoner Pearce, in my presence—from what he told me, I questioned Revell when she came in—she, after some time acknowledged that she ran down and drew the wine the moment I sat down to dinner, brought it up in a jug, and asked Pearce to get the bottles, which he did, and she filled them with wine, for him to take it home—she said she had never done so before—but in a short time she said she did it once before, when I was bottling a cask of wine, Pearce told her it was very good, and she said she had got him some—this led to other things—I had her sister's house searched, and some of my property was found, which was given up before the Magistrate—I will take Pearce into my service again.
WILLIAM MORRIS ( police-constable M 93.) On Monday last I was on duty, and saw Pearce putting some bottles into his pocket—I took him with these—I took him to his master, who identified the property.
Revell's Defence. Pearce came to me, and said his master was bottling some wine, and he should like to taste some—I went down and drew some—I brought it up and tasted it, it was very good—he said I durst not drink—I said, "Get a bottle;" and it would not hold all—he fetched another, and that was filled, and then the third.
Pearce. She gave it to me to take home to my sister, who was very ill.
PEARCE— GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Five Days.
REVELL— GUILTY . Aged 35.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18360919-2232]
2232. MARY NEWLAND was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of September, 1 work-box, value 2 l. 10 s.; 2 pairs of spectacles, value 5 s. 1 spectacle-case, value 6 d.; 1 knitting-roll, value 2 s.; 1 fruit-knife, value 3 s. 1/2 yard of silk, value 2 s.; 3 yards of lace, value 5 s.; three yards of trimming, value 1 s.; and two half-crowns, the goods and monies of James Strong Probert, her master.
JAMES STRONG PROBERT . I live in Oxley-place, Lambeth, and am a cabinet-maker. The prisoner was my servant—on the 15th of September she went away, leaving my door open—I missed a box, which was made in my own shop—this is it, and all the contents are mine—the whole is worth about 3 l. 5 s.—the box had not been opened, I believe, as the key was in my possession.
FREDERICK GILES . I am shopman to Mr. Russell, a pawnbroker, of High-Holborn. On the 17th of September the prisoner came with a lady's work-box, and asked 2 s. in pledge on it—I asked whose it was, she said her own, that she bought it in Dublin—asked for the key, and she said she had lost it on board the steamer she came in, at the St. Katharine's Docks—I sent for a policeman, who took her.
GUILTY . Aged 20— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18360919-2233]
2233. ROBERT ISAACS and THOMAS WHITE were indicted for stealing, on the 10th of September, 2 seat-irons, value 8 d.; 4 awls, value 6 d.; 1 pump-stick, value 3 d.; 1 fore-part iron, value 4 d.; and 1 drill iron, value 3 d.; the goods of James Howard.
JAMES HOWARD . I live in East-street Lambeth, and keep a shoemaker's stall in St. George's-road. About three o'clock on the 10th of September, I went to my stall after dinner—my window was safe when I went away—when I came back a pane of glass was cut out—I had left all these tools safe when I went away—they were gone when I came back, and the prisoners were at the window—I found these tools on White.
EDWARD LAWRENCE ( Police-constable L 182.) About three o'clock on that afternoon I took the prisoners into custody—I searched them, and found these two articles on White, and these two awls on Isaacs—these other five articles were given me by the prosecutor, which he took from one of their pockets.
Guilty.
(The indictment also charged that both the prisoners had been previously convicted.)
WILLIAM ALDERMAN . I got this certificate of the prisoners' former conviction from the Clerk of the Peace for Survey—( read)—the prisoners are the persons who were convicted.
ISAACS— GUILTY . Aged 12.
WHITE— GUILTY . Aged 12.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18360919-2234]
2234. JOHN POVEY was indicted for embezzlement.
NATHANIEL FREDERICK OKEY . I keep the Hero of Waterloo, Waterloo-road. The prisoner was my pot-boy—I sent him, on the 3rd of September to Messrs. Cotton and Allen, in Cornwall-road, for an account, amounting to 11 s. 4 d.—he never returned, and has never paid it to me.
THOMAS EDWARDS . I am clerk to Cotton and Allen, of Cornwall-road, iron-founders. On Saturday, the 3rd of September, I paid the prisoner 11 s. 4 d. for his master, Mr. Okey.
Prisoner. I took the money and made use of a small portion—I had some money owing to me—I could not get it on Saturday or Sunday, but I should have got it on Tuesday.
GEORGE WILSON ( police-constable L 162.) I took the prisoner on the 6th of September, and asked him if he knew Mr. Okey—as we were going along, he said, "It was only 11 s. 4 d.; that I took."
Guilty.
(The indictment also charged a previous conviction.)
THOMAS EDWARDS ( police-constable L 31.) I produce the certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from the office—( read) the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18360919-2235]
2235. HENRY DENHAM was indicted for an assault on Jane Bassett. with an intent to violate her.
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Confined One Year, and to find sureties.
ADMIRALTY CASES.
Before Mr. Justice Bosanquet.
[trialAccount t18360919-2236]
2236. THOMAS BLAKELEY was indicted for feloniously cutting and wounding Augustus Cæsar, with intent to kill and murder him.—2 other COUNTS, stating his intent to be, to disable or to do him some grievous bodily harm.
NOT GUILTY, being of unsound mind.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18360919-2237]
2237. WILLIAM JOHNSON was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of August, 6 silver dollars, the monies of Richard Bell.
NOT GUILTY
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18360919-2238]
2238. NELSON CORNER was indicted for an assault.
GUILTY . Aged 36.— Fined £100.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, THE 24 TH OF OCTOBER.
